Wed , 18 Sep 2019 09:02:36 +0000https : **26;4234;TOOLONG ? p=52502 ... views expressed in this article are not necessarily those of The South African <p> " It is our land . We are the custodians. " -- Felix Shanungu , President of the Zambia National Community Resources Board ( ZNCRB ) . <p> They have withdrawn their signatures to all the hunting permits in their areas and have refused to sign any others . This will stop any trophy hunting in the future unless the government comes to the table with money in hand . <p> According to Felix Shanungo , the communities have received no concession fees since 2016 and no hunting revenue since last year . By law , the communities are entitled to 20% of the concession fees and 50% of the hunting revenue . The chiefs who run the communities are owed a 5% share of both . <p> While the press release states that they will stop all hunting going forward , Mr. Shanungo advised that hunts already underway will be allowed to be completed but that all new hunts will be stopped . The CRB has been in talks with the hunting companies to warn them about this and to get them to put pressure on the Zambian government . He added that the communities do not want to penalise the hunting companies who have paid but want the pressure to galvanise government into action . <p> He said that it will be impossible for the communities to continue patrolling and protecting against poaching as people have not been paid their salaries in months . <h> The communities ' demands <p> The communities have two demands : To allow the hunting operators to pay the CRBs their share directly and that the concession fees must be re-negotiated for a higher share . <p> Various hunting outfits claim that trophy hunting brings USD200 million into the sub-Saharan Africa economy . This figure was published in the academic journal Biological Conservation and is often used to defend hunting , a claim hotly contested by conservationists who contend that less than 3% of hunting revenues actually go to communities . The same paper claimed that this figure was accumulated by 18 500 hunters . In comparison , a World Bank report estimated that close to 33.8 million people visit the region ( mainly for wildlife tourism ) and contribute USD36 billion . Most tourists who come to visit for the wildlife do not realise that hunting is allowed in these countries ; it is believed that Africa 's reputation will suffer if this fact was more widely known . <p> The wildlife areas in Zambia are divided into the National Parks ( where no hunting is allowed ) and game management areas ( GMA ) which act as a buffer between the parks , farmlands and private hunting reserves . Legally , there has to be revenue sharing from hunting and concession fees with the communities in the GMAs -- this is called Community Based Natural Resources Management ( CBNRM ) . In order to ensure the money is delivered and managed , several CRBs were created . <p> With growing concerns over biological collapse in the time of the sixth mass extinction , it is only a matter of time before global pressure phases out hunting all together . It seems better for the countries in question to determine their own phasing out process . This will allow them to focus on community based eco-tourism where revenue can go directly to communities , and to expand the tourism sector versus allowing the killing of some of the most spectacular treasures we have on this planet . <p> &gt;If Malema becomes President : What the EFF wants to do with your moneyhttps : **34;4262;TOOLONG ... Economic Freedom Fighters ( EFF ) , led by Julius Malema , will set out its economic policy in what is expected to be an aggressive and engaging parliamentary debate on the role of the state in South Africa 's future growth or decline . <p> It is one of the enduring and positive legacies of Jackson Mthembu 's tenure as ANC Chief Whip that every Tuesday afternoon , during the parliamentary sitting , MPs get the opportunity to engage in a battle of ideas as political parties each get a turn -- on a proportional rotational basis -- to set down a topical policy issue for debate . <p> This afternoon , it is the EFF 's turn to propose the debate topic , which is set to be introduced by its leader , Julius Malema . The topic , as proposed by the EFF is , " Building state capacity through insourcing of government and all state-owned companies ' workers . " <p> Much as that may seem many people 's worst nightmare , it is the stuff EFF dreams are made of , several Red Beret wish list items rolled into one somewhat laboriously worded magic carpet ready to take us on to a statist wonderland . <h> Julius Malema to address Parliament on EFF economic policies <p> Malema , his deputy Floyd Shivambu and the EFF as a whole , take these economic debates very seriously , so expect them to be very well prepared . EFF policy documents lay out in some detail how , were it ever to come to power , the EFF will build a resourceful state owning a monopoly on everything from construction and pharmaceuticals to land . <p> Expect also to hear lots of attacks on the free market from the Reds this afternoon , as well as white monopoly capital and Stellenbosch -- the concept it represents in an EFF world view , not the actual quaint oak-lined town . <p> The EFF is likely to point out , empirically and correctly , that inequality in South Africa has deepened since 1994 . More controversially , it will blame the extent to which the ANC government has favoured the free market ( spoiler alert -- watch out , " neo-liberalism " will be in the firing line ) for this inequality . <p> Do not expect to hear anything , though , on EFF finances , the VBS calamity which destroyed the tenuous hold of the rural poor in the country 's extreme north on financial stability , but which , according to investigative work by the Daily Maverick , paid for some lavish shopping excursions by EFF toadies . <h> Democratic Alliance set to hit back <p> The EFF 's statist views are , of course , anathema to the DA , which will , no doubt , ask the EFF for a list of things state-owned entities do right . <p> The DA is likely to list all the state does wrong , starting with Eskom , sovereign debt ratings , the SAA , Denel , the SABC and the bailouts which , through the failure of the South African state , bleed dry the very funds a developmental state would have to rely upon to save the poor from destitution . <p> If the DA were to find it within themselves to make these points in a calm and considered manner , as quizzing rather than blasting the EFF 's ideological basis , today 's debate could serve a purpose , namely to help move South African political parties away from the thought silos where each policy discussion is an echo chamber with no-one listening and trying to understand the opposing party 's view . <p> That , after all , is the aim of debate for the intelligent -- it is an opportunity to listen whether you can understand and learn anything from your opponent 's view so that you gain and grow in the process . It is supposed to be an exercise in listening , first and foremost , not an exercise in shouting . <p> What the EFF will have to do in today 's debate is to set out what its belief in the South African civil service as the catalyst rather than the wrecking ball of economic growth is based on . <h> EFF 's land policy to come under the microscope <p> If its opponents do their job , they should also be pushed on their land policy . Senior police officers did a thorough presentation on exactly what was planned for the amnesty . <h> Amnesty on unlicensed firearms , explained <p> The aim of the amnesty would be to ensure that fewer illegal and unlicensed firearms would be in circulation in South Africa , in order to lessen violent crime , and to afford people to hand in firearms they no longer wanted for destruction in an orderly manner . <p> Anyone who can prove ownership or possession of a firearm over an extended period can license that firearm as his or her own provided the firearm does not belong to someone else and the correct procedure is followed . <p> The police then presented an extensive plan to the committee on how the amnesty would be administered , including , amongst others , the transportation of the firearms , checking the history of the firearm and the history of the person who handed it in , the requirements those police officers who would have to deal with the firearms would have to comply with to help administer the amnesty , the documents and checklists required , the identification of one storage facility per province where firearms can be stored before destruction , the requirements and guidelines for the destruction of the firearms , and the use of ballistic tests to check whether the firearm had been used for committing a crime in the past so that evidence of crime is not destroyed . <p> The police presentation ended off on the hopeful and confident note that although it was a challenge to conduct such an amnesty , the SAPS believed it had foreseen and overcome all expected challenges and were ready to act . <h> Members of parliament lack faith in SAPS <p> This was not to be , however , as the police faced a barrage of criticism from the politicians . <p> The trusted by the community and <p> Police stations are so poorly run , maintained and secured that it is a bad idea to keep those firearms which are handed in at police stations , lest the police meeting there and then without the police given any time to react or answer questions . <p> In closing the meeting , Shaikh said ratification of an amnesty would be discussed at another time , but not ANC held its first parliamentary caucus lekgotla ( planning meeting ) this weekend as a tacit nod to the need for improvement in its levels of parliamentary participation and functioning , but its feedback on the results of the meeting showed that the focus was elsewhere . <p> After the governing party lost nineteen seats to recede from 249 seats to 230 in the elections in May this year , its proportional funding decreased to the extent that it had to let many of its administrative and research staff in parliament go , which raises new capacity risks . <h> ANC members slammed for parliamentary tardiness <p> As for many ANC MPs , they are often accused by members of opposition parties of arriving unprepared for parliamentary committee meetings , not having read the relevant documentation and merely kowtowing to power -- not taking the time and effort to question officials and ministers as diligently as their oversight role requires . <p> It is also alleged that many ANC MPs are tardy about fulfilling their constituency duties . The Captive Elephant Indaba was held to consider the plight of captive elephants and provide policy recommendations for the future . <p> " Although we have seen a distinct improvement in the number of captive elephants in South Africa , we still have 95 elephants in 22 captive facilities , " says Brett Mitchell of Elephant Reintegration Trust . " The number of facilities offering elephant back riding decreased by 80% over the last five years , largely as a result of public pressure . " <p> Elephant back riding on the Garden Route , South Africa <p> Dr Joyce Poole of Elephant Voices expressed her views . <p> " Elephants are highly intelligent and social creatures with large brains capable of cognitive processing , self-awareness and mourning loss , and display a complex and extensive repertoire of communication . They are large and long-lived mammals reaching ages similar to us humans . " <p> These are traits unsuitable to captive conditions , where elephants are often confined to " small and barren camps , offering little to no opportunity for exercise , mobility behavioural challenges , or choices over where to go or with whom to spend time , " adds Dr Keith Lindsay of Amboseli Elephant Research Project . <p> One of the aims of the Indaba was to bring awareness to the plight of captive elephants and the cruel methods of training used to break these animals into submission to facilitate interaction . To achieve perpetual submissiveness , they are often also put on the Gonadotropin Releasing Hormone , which reduces their testosterone levels but has severe side including causing genital deformities . <p> " When subjected to genocide , imprisonment , cultural destruction , enslavement , loss of homeland , torture , and war , or in the language of elephant managers and conservationists , culls , translocation , captivity training , human-elephant conflict , and crop protection , both humans and elephants experience psychological and social breakdown . Elephant trauma survivors can only begin to heal when elephant ways of life are reinstated . " <p> During the Convention on International Trade in Endangered Species of Wild Fauna and Flora ( CITES ) CoP18 meeting in Geneva in August this year , a majority vote restricted the live elephant trade . CITES now only allows the live trade of wild elephants within their natural and historic range in Africa , except in exceptional or emergency circumstances . <p> " This means that wild baby elephants can no longer be ripped away from their families and sold to zoos and captive facilities , which is a great start , " states Audrey Delsink of HSI Africa . <p> " However , the only truly ' appropriate and acceptable ' conditions for this iconic and keystone species are in their natural habitat , " says Lindsay . <p> Baby elephant captured from the wild in Zimbabwe and held captive in Jinan Zoo in China . " <p> **43;4661;TOOLONG ... ready , set , go for process to remove Public Protector Mkhwebanehttps : **30;4706;TOOLONG ... Mon , 09 Sep 2019 17:10:14 +0000https : **26;4738;TOOLONG ? p=52038 ... all goes according to plan , Parliament will start working on a process to remove those heading Section 9 institutions in South Africa , but who do not meet expectations . That could set a way to get rid of Public Protector Busisiwe Mkhwebane , should it be the wish of the majority in the National Assembly . <p> At this stage , there simply does not exist a way to get rid of the Public Protector should he/she not make the grade . It is a current shortcoming of our ever evolving constitutional order . What Parliament has now embarked upon is to fix that procedural shortcoming . <h> Parliament decides when and how to remove a Public Protector <p> Hence , this week the parliamentary rules committee will meet to discuss a new draft rule crafted by DA chief whip John Steenhuisen . It is quite a detailed piece of work , but at its core it lists three reasons why Parliament can decide to remove a Public Protector . These are incapacity ( if the Public Protector becomes too ill to do the job ) , incompetence or misconduct . <p> The first step to get rid of the Public Protector under the proposed new draft rules is that an enabling motion must be passed by majority vote in the National Assembly , after which an investigation must be undertaken by a multiparty committee of MPs . <p> The proposal currently is that half of the MPs on the committee must be opposition MPs , which is a part of the proposal which is very unlikely to survive , given the South African parliamentary principle of proportionality . <p> The multiparty committee has the option of being assisted and informed by an independent expert panel which may for instance include retired judges . <h> Simple majority or two-thirds majority ? <p> Once the multiparty committee has made its recommendation whether to retain or remove the Public Protector , the National Assembly votes on the recommendation . It is not explicitly stated in the draft rule whether a simple majority or a two-thirds majority is required to remove the Public Protector , which , if the draft rule were to be adopted as it stands , would mean only a simple majority would be adopted . <p> It is expected that the parliamentary rules committee will eventually largely agree to the draft rule , possibly with some changes , most likely to honour the principle of proportionality and perhaps to set a higher bar for the majority required to remove the Public Protector . <p> If and when such a rule is adopted , it is no given that it will be used to remove Busisiwe Mkhwebane , the current incumbent . She has the firm support of the EFF and the ANC may well be loath to expose and test the dividing lines on her future fate within the governing party . <p> But once a procedure is established by which Parliament can act , a shortcoming in our constitutional setup would have been addressed and the efforts to remove her can begin . <p> &gt;A Cape Town united forces Ramaphosa 's hand on femicidehttps : **31;4766;TOOLONG ... Mother City of Cape Town lived up to its name and its reputation on Thursday , as it rose up against the recent upsurge in femicide and violence against women and children . <h> Spontaneous demonstrations fill Cape Town 's streets <p> The great and unusual thing about Thursday 's events , were that they were largely a spontaneous outpouring of community feeling rather than an organised event by an organised , single-issue group . <p> This was evident in the Cape Town city centre by mid-morning , where small groups were gathering at well-known city landmarks such as the Company Gardens , the St. George 's Cathedral , Parliament , the Naspers Centre , the Convention Centre and the St George 's Mall . <p> All they had in common was that they wore black , that they were committed in demanding action on rape and femicide and that some of them had individually made placards with uniquely crafted slogans on them . <p> They came in all shapes and sizes , political persuasions , social backgrounds , genders , races , classes and languages . Schoolchildren , old grannies , hipsters , working class people with a life of sorrow crafted on their weary faces , career professionals perhaps more at home in the corporate and office environment than being on the street . <p> And they were many . Really , really many , so that when word went out that all had to meet at the gates of Parliament , the nerve centre of our democracy , they filled the streets , city blocks of them , in their thousands and brought the city to a standstill . <p> The diversity of the crowd , united by their disgust at the attacks on the vulnerable , was evidenced by the songs they sang in protest . Much as the usual struggle favourites like Siyaya and Senzeni na ? were dusted off , some Michael Jackson hits were also sung by a crowd which included many Coloured and white Capetonians probably taking part in their first mass protest . <h> ' Where is President Cyril Ramaphosa ? Tue , 03 Sep 2019 06:29:27 +0000https : **26;4916;TOOLONG ? p=51665 ... will see a very unusual event in Parliament when desperate interest groups lobby politicians against the controversial Copyright Bill -- a piece of legislation which could change every South African 's life profoundly . <p> It is a classic example of the road to hell being paved with good intention -- but still being very much the road to hell regardless . <p> It is also yet another example of how stubbornly the ANC can ignore expert advice and write bad law , thereby risking much financial loss , and how the current governing party excels at building ever more deeply unneeded bureaucratic institutions with secretaries , staff and even more high-pay , low octane civil service positions the country simply does not have the budget for ( why do the words National Health Insurance suddenly come up in screaming red letters ? ) . <h> Details of the contentious Copyright Bill <p> The Copyright Bill has been passed by both houses of parliament . It is a complicated beast of 67 pages of legalese , much of it , one is sad to say , quite poorly drafted and vague . It aims to update South African copyright law for the first time since 1978 , for instance bringing it into the digital age . <p> The effects of the bill on specifically the South African publishing and film industry will be discussed by those affected by it and by the parliamentary portfolio committee on arts and culture today . Considering that the horse has bolted , parliamentary , it can only be a <p> Clearly two elements were very important to the drafters of the bill , and to the ANC when they voted for it . Both elements speak of great kindness and dangerous ignorance . These two elements are that indigenous traditional scientific know-how and cultural intellectual property must be safeguarded from exploitation and that the cost of academic study would be so much lower if students could just copy books instead of buying them . <p> To deal with the first element , the bill creates what it calls " collection societies " for royalties on indigenous knowledge and cultural performance common to a specific group of people . <p> Only those who bother to belong to these collection societies will benefit . <p> The cynical might well wonder whether yet another cesspool of corruption , inefficiency , bail-outs , mediocrity and self-enrichment is being created -- more so because the mandate entrusted to them is so complicated , the way they will come into being so unclear and exactly how they will be funded so unmentioned . Eventual regulations will apparently decide these important issues , far from the glare of the open parliamentary process . <p> Incidentally , I pity whosoever has the task of deciding on the competing claims to cultural appropriation and its monetary implications . It opens the door for such greed and self-righteous opportunism . What a nightmare to administer . <h> Further strain on the budget <p> The bill also stipulated the strengthening ( read : enlargement ) of the Copyright Tribunal and then states helpfully that any additional costs " will be accommodated within the current budget " . Quite how , and what will have to be cut from the budget as a result -- the budget being a finite and limited resource -- is not stated . Easy does it , apparently . <p> To deal with the second element , free copying of work , for instance academic work , produced in South Africa is made far easier and will become more common . The same does not go for such work produced outside of the country . There could be many terms for this , one of which is " moronic " . At a time when Government claims it wants to " decolonise " education , by the stroke of a pen it will re-colonise it because no-one would be so daft as to publish such books locally at what would amount to a catastrophic financial loss , so all wisdom will come courtesy of foreign input . <p> Smart work , is it not , from the ANC majority in Parliament ? <h> Predicted losses of R2.1 billion <p> That same grouping , which , in plain terms , have now been repeatedly told by the Constitutional Court to start doing its parliamentary job in a brighter way , has also failed to grasp the contradiction between the tightening of control regarding indigenous knowledge and cultural products on the one hand , and the relaxation of same with regards to academic publications on the other . The less said about thoughtfulness and logic , the better . <p> The publications industry and the auditing firm Price Waterhouse Coopers have done an audit which found that the country will lose R2,1 billion and many jobs directly in the publications and film industry in the first year should the bill be published in its current form . It has argued that the will and effort to create -- especially in the academic sphere -- will dissipate . It has not stated the obvious -- that the best academics are , in circumstances which might be unintended but certainly not unforeseen , in effect incentivised to leave the country if they want to publish , given that sustainable academic publication in South Africa will be dead as a dodo should the President sign the bill into law . <p> Ramaphosa now has three options . He can either sign it into law , which could prove disastrous , or just do nothing , which will mean the bill will gather dust and not be effected , as the so called Secrecy Bill has done since 2011 , or he can send this wretched bill back to Parliament for reconsideration . <p> The country waits . <p> &gt;ANC bigwigs face spectre of arrest as state funds ' diverted'https : **29;4944;TOOLONG ... Thu , 29 Aug 2019 10:51:46 +0000https : **26;4975;TOOLONG ? p=51432 ... senior ANC members could be headed for trouble with the law after a trade union made serious allegations about the diversion of state funds for party political interests . <p> The tensions between the ANC and the very strong parliamentary branch of the National Education , Health and Allied Workers ' Union ( Nehawu ) is one of the most visible signs of the stress the governing party is under as a direct result of its heavy losses in the general election held in May this year . <h> ANC : A loss in votes means a loss in parliamentary staff <p> The ANC lost 19 seats , falling from 249 to 230 seats in the National Assembly . Because everything in South African politics is calculated proportionally , there is a concomitant loss of ANC parliamentary employees subsidised by the taxpayer . Those state funds are forked out proportionally to political parties so that they can function effectively in Parliament , with the argument that it strengthens democracy . <p> The May election results mean parties which grew , like the EFF ( 19 seat growth from 25 to 44 ) , Freedom Front Plus ( 6 seat growth from 4 to 10 ) ) , IFP ( 3 seat growth from 10 to 13 ) and Good ( 2 seat growth from a zero base ) can all employ more parliamentary staff , courtesy of the taxpayer and the provisions of the law . <p> The DA , which shrunk by 5 seats from 89 to 84 , has not fired staff , but has suspended appointments in some positions . <p> But the jobs bloodbath is real among ANC parliamentary staff , where almost a quarter ( 124 out of 460 ANC parliamentary staff ) are facing the axe due to the voters ' verdict . Wed , 28 Aug 2019 13:00:05 +0000https : **26;5038;TOOLONG ? p=51376 ... South Africans are progressing up the jobs ladder , but slowly , according to the national employment equity figures released by the Department of Labour . <p> Stripped of veneer , the rows and rows of numbers reveal a stark reality -- some jobs and some provinces are more equal than others , and the higher the qualification and payment levels , the higher the white representation . <h> Management positions <p> The lower the qualifications and pay , the higher the black representation . Black South Africans are strongly represented at all levels in the public sector , but less so in the private sector . <p> Of all the figures , 44% was submitted from Gauteng , 21% from the Western Cape , 15% from KwaZulu-Natal , 5% from Mpumalanga and 15% from the other five provinces . In total , about 7,5 million employees and more than 27 000 employers were covered , mostly in manufacturing , agriculture , wholesale trade and construction . <p> In top management , 65% of the positions countrywide are held by whites , and 77% of the country 's top management jobs are held by men . In state employment , 76% of top managers in the public sector are black , whereas 69% of private sector top managers are white . <p> In the private sector , 77% of top management positions are occupied by men , and in the public sector , male top management dominance comes to 67% . <p> In agriculture , 85% of top management is white , in retail it is 75% and in wholesale the figure is 70% . Black South Africans constitute 77% of top management in provincial government and 76% in provincial government . <p> Senior management countrywide is 54% white , 23% black , 11% Indian , 8% Coloured , 3% foreign and 65% male . In the state sector , 71% of senior managers are black , and in the private sector , 60% of senior management is white . <p> The province with the highest number of white senior managers is the Western Cape at 62% followed by the Free State on 59% and the Eastern Cape with 57% . <p> As far as professionally qualified workers go , whites narrowly rule the roost nationally on 40% , followed by blacks ( 37% ) , Indians ( 9% ) and Coloureds ( 9% ) . In the state , 65% of professionally qualified workers are black , whereas in the private sector 45% of professionally qualified workers are white . <p> Provincially , 85% of professionally qualified workers in Limpopo are black , as are 62% in Mpumalanga , 55% in the Eastern Cape and 52% in North West . <p> Moving to skilled technical workers , 63% nationally are black , 18% are white , 11% are Coloured , 5% are Indian and 1% is foreign . In Limpopo , 92% of skilled technical workers are black . In Mpumalanga , the figure is 80% and in North West 76% . <p> The national picture for semi-skilled workers is that they are 75% black , 12% Coloured , 5% white , 3% Indian and 2% foreign . <p> The unskilled workforce is made up of 83% blacks , 11% Coloured , 4% foreigners , 1% whites and 1% Indians , with black South Africans now the majority in every province . <p> **43;5066;TOOLONG ... the IFP have a future without Mangosuthu Buthelezi ? https : **29;5111;TOOLONG ... Minister Tito Mboweni has instructed government departments to cut roughly 5% from their budgets from the burgeoning civil service salary bill rather than from any money earmarked for projects or service delivery . <p> Whilst this is the course long recommended by economists and other experts , it will heighten tensions between Mboweni , trade unions and certain powerful forces in the ANC who refuse to agree to job cuts in order to aid much needed austerity measures . <h> A three-year plan : Details of the ordered salary cuts <p> National Treasury has instructed government departments to cut 5% of spending this year , 6% next year and 7% the year after . The austerity measures follow in the wake of rising unemployment , no possible cuts in government grants , poor economic growth , increased state borrowing , spiralling debt service costs and the possibility that ratings agency Moody 's may change the sovereign rating to junk status . <p> It is going to prove a hard bullet to bite . In the police service alone , 23 000 jobs may be lost , which will be deeply unpopular in a population reeling from an unceasing crime wave . Were the job cuts Mboweni advocates b not to be done , a further shortage of R30 billion on the medium term budget will be incurred , which South Africa simply can not afford . <p> The country is now picking the bitter fruit of many years of lackadaisical political mismanagement and unwillingness to take tough but necessary decisions . Over the last ten years , civil servants , egged on by irresponsible and militant unions , saw their salaries increase by an average of 11,2% per year ( almost double the inflation rate ) with very little concomitant increase in productivity or service excellence to the general public . <h> Mboweni likely to lose the popularity contest <p> The expectation of government departments is that they now grant below inflation increases with above inflation increases in the contributions civil servants make to for instance medical funds . Is he involved in the political dark arts , and is he going to explain himself ? <p> The plot in this drama thickens , the plot in this drama sickens , and the curtain has certainly not come down on it yet . <p> &gt;South Africa pushes for trade in endangered wildlifehttps : **31;5450;TOOLONG ... Tue , 20 Aug 2019 10:55:58 +0000https : **26;5483;TOOLONG ? p=50930 ... views in expressed in this article are not necessarily those of The South African <p> In a submission to the eighteenth conference of the parties ( CoP18 ) to CITES ( the Convention on International Trade in Endangered Species of Wild Fauna and Flora ) to be held in Switzerland later this month , the countries argue for a major overhaul in the way in which the organisation operates . <p> They believe that they should be allowed to sell threatened species of wildlife anywhere in the world in the same way that mineral resources and mass-produced plastic trinkets are traded on global commercial markets . <p> The changes to CITES they are asking for would pave the way for southern African countries to legally sell stockpiled ivory and rhino horn , which would put immense pressure on wild animal species already under severe threat of long-term extinction . <h> Unfair treatment ? <p> One of the proposed amendments would make it easier to move species from one CITES Appendix to another . Animals and plants listed in the organisation 's Appendix I are effectively barred from commercial international trade while those on Appendix II can only be traded under special circumstances . <p> The countries suggesting the changes claim to be victims of discriminatory CITES procedures , contend that trade prohibitions have not put an end to the illegal trade in wildlife products like rhino horn , and claim that " more and more species that are well protected in southern Africa and demonstrably sustainably used " are being " targeted " by " proposals from outside the southern African region " for transfer to Appendix I. <p> Making it faster and easier to move species like elephants and rhinos from Appendices that restrict trade is clearly one of the intended outcomes and the authors of the document argue that " progress has been extremely slow " on this front . <p> Other recommendations would bring CITES more in line with the World Trade Organisation 's General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade ( GATT ) , a neo-liberal regulatory regime that facilitates unfettered access to global markets for commercial goods and services . South Africa and its partner countries claim that CITES " provides for discrimination " and offers " no provisions against unfair trade practices " . They evidently believe that what they consider to be " discriminatory and trade-restricting measures " enacted by CITES could be successfully challenged under GATT rules . <p> According to Mary Rice , the Executive Director of the Environmental Investigation Agency ( EIA ) , " the relationship between GATT and CITES has been explored at multiple CoPs between 2006 and 2013 . The parties to CITES rejected all recommendations submitted through that process , so we believe that no review is needed " . <p> The proposal is n't the only one pushing for trade liberalisation . Namibia wants the current protections on its population of southern white rhinos to be lowered , Zambia , Botswana , Namibia and Zimbabwe want to be allowed to sell ivory internationally and Eswatini argues that it should be permitted to trade in rhino horn . <p> But not all African governments agree . In sharp contrast to Southern African proposals , ten countries further to the north , including Kenya , Ivory Coast and Gabon want all African elephants to be moved to Appendix I in order to protect the species as a whole . Currently , elephant populations in South Africa , Botswana , Zimbabwe and Namibia are listed in Appendix II . <p> Conservationists contend that it makes little sense to classify animals of the same species in different Appendices just because they happen to be found within the boundaries of a particular country , especially if those species are migratory and in overall decline across the continent as is the case for elephants . <h> Anti-poor ? <p> Charges that CITES is neo-imperialist , neo-colonialist and " definitely not pro-poor " , because its anti-trade policies deprive rural communities of the opportunity to " realize the full economic value of their wildlife resources " and starve conservation programmes of funds would sound less hollow if they did n't come from governments that have consistently shown themselves to be deeply corrupt , captured by powerful corporate interests and ineffectual at addressing some of the most basic grievances of their citizens from crippling poverty to the non-delivery of services . <p> The sale of wildlife products in these countries is much more likely to benefit political elites and their cronies than the rural poor whose poverty the authors ' of the document invoke . <p> In a similar vein , their assertion that trophy hunting in southern Africa is " well-regulated " is extremely dubious in light of South Africa 's sordid record of canned lion hunting and the illegal killing of Cecil , Zimbabwe 's most famous lion in 2015 , to name just two relatively high-profile examples . <p> As has become routine for ardent supporters of trophy hunting -- an elite activity if ever there was one -- they ignore the fact that non-consumptive ecotourism provides consistently higher economic benefits for a larger base of people than commercial hunting . What 's more , there is **26;5511;TOOLONG that killing wildlife for trophies is not possible without detrimental effects on herds and populations . <h> Sustainable use or profit-driven exploitation ? <p> In essence , the countries proposing these changes to CITES are upset about the fact that current rules prohibit them from deriving profits from wild animals which they consider to be valuable products that they should be entitled to harvest and sell however they see fit . They consider their autonomy to be unfairly restricted by regulations imposed on them by " ever so many armchair critics and self-proclaimed experts " . <p> At the most fundamental level , what is at the heart of this matter is a long-standing dispute over what is meant by the ' sustainable use ' of wildlife . In a modern-day equivalent of enclosure , the countries proposing the changes to CITES , consider all wildlife found within their borders to be their property and a commodity that they should be allowed to produce , harvest and sell competitively in markets around the world just as they are permitted to trade in ore and agricultural produce . <p> They contend that they are able to do so " sustainably " -- in other words without doing harm to or causing the extinction of species . Concerns about the conservation of wild animals in their natural habitat feature little in this view . To them , there is no discernible difference between species of wildlife and domesticated livestock , and they insist that both should be exploitable as products for financial profit . <p> South Africa is one of the best examples of this philosophy in action . Over the last decade or so , the government , guided by economists promoting extreme free market policies and the unrestricted commodification and commercialisation of nature , has succeeded in crafting laws and regulations that explicitly lay out this interpretation of ' sustainable use ' , for instance in the case of lions and rhinos . <p> The government-supported industry of breeding lions in captivity in South Africa provides an illustration of the outcomes of this philosophy . Supposedly proud of its global wildlife conservation status , the country now hosts more of these caged and commodified lions than live in its national parks and nature reserves . <p> The problem is that wild animals are not the same as commercial goods and lions bred in captivity for the sole purpose of becoming targets for wealthy trophy hunters and a ready supply of bones for the market in Traditional Chinese medicine are neither capable of surviving in the wild nor have any conservation value whatsoever . In fact , one could argue that they are no longer truly lions in an ecological sense . <h> An extinction crisis <p> Arguments by South Africa and others to expand international wildlife trade must be seen in the context of dire warnings from the scientific community . Earlier this year , a devastating UN report highlighted evidence that the exploitation of wildlife is a major threat to a global ecosystem that is being ravaged by species extinction at an extremely alarming and historically unprecedented rate . In light of this , international conservation organisations like Humane Society International are calling on world leaders to resist proposals for changes to CITES that would facilitate trade . <p> While conservationists agree that the meaning of ' sustainable use ' needs to be clarified within CITES , they do so for fundamentally different reasons to those offered by the authors of the proposal . They argue that wild animal species and nature as a whole can not be treated as supposedly renewable resources and a form of capital that can be exploited indefinitely in simplistic economic terms . Any interpretation of ' sustainable use ' must be founded on ecological science , not on economic bottom lines . <p> Global biodiversity , healthy natural ecosystems and intact wilderness landscapes are not just beautiful and part of every human 's heritage , they are the very basis on which civilisation is built . They are not just ' nice-to-haves ' , but fundamental requirements for the survival of our own species . They are a common good that provides immeasurable benefits for all people across nation state boundaries . Their intrinsic worth lies in the fact that they are our planet 's life support system . Do we really want to trade them away in exchange for short-term monetary gains which will end up mostly in the pockets of domestic elites ? <p> " While CITES does have its shortcomings , " says the EIA 's Shruti Suresh , " it is currently the primary internationally binding treaty dedicated to addressing trade in animal and plant species of concern and we need to ensure that the precautionary approach is at the heart of any changes to the CITES framework " . <p> Given the current extinction crisis we should do everything to protect endangered species , not expand ways to exploit them to their greatest commercial potential and it is extremely short-sighted and irresponsible for South Africa and other countries to make the proposals that would diminish CITES ' effectiveness . <p> &gt;This is South Africa 's best-run municipality , financially speakinghttps : **31;5539;TOOLONG ... Tue , 20 Aug 2019 08:37:45 +0000https : **26;5572;TOOLONG ? p=50918 ... Treasury has announced that the Drakenstein municipality , based in the town of Paarl in the Western Cape , has been named ' financially the best run municipality ' in the country for the most recent municipal financial year . <p> The Western Cape has yet again been named the top province for municipal financial management , with the Cape Town metro and -- perhaps somewhat surprisingly -- the municipality of George , which has recently had terrible publicity about alleged corruption and mismanagement , singled <h> Politics of the Drakenstein <p> Drakenstein has been governed by the DA for many years and remained a rock solid DA stronghold in this year 's general election . <p> It is the municipality with the second largest population in the Western Cape ( Cape Town obviously being the largest by a country mile ) . In addition to Paarl , Drakenstein also serves the town of Wellington , the hamlets of Saron , Hermon and Gouda , as well as the many surrounding farms . <p> It is the second year in a row Drakenstein has come out tops as what the national government terms the most financially healthy municipality in South Africa . <p> The municipality finds itself in a great neighbourhood for South African municipal excellence -- it is bordered by the municipalities of Cape Town , Stellenbosch , Swartland ( centred on Malmesbury ) and Breede Valley ( centred on Worcester ) . <p> Each of these municipalities is excellently managed and between them often scoops just about every municipal award the country offers . In this they are strengthened by generally very well qualified professional staff , comparatively low staff turnover , a near absence of notable corruption scandals and finally enviable political stability -- each of the four councils features a very comfortable DA majority , and can rightly be referred to as DA heartland . <h> Criteria used to measure the financial health of municipalities <p> According to the National Treasury , the criteria used to measure the financial health of a municipality includes eight key measures namely : <p> Cash as a percentage of operating expenditure ; <p> Persistence of negative cash balances ; <p> Overspending of original operating budgets ; <p> Underspending of original capital budgets ; <p> Debtors as a percentage of own revenue ; <p> Year-on-year growth in debtors ; <p> Creditors as a percentage of cash and investments ; and <p> Reliance on national and provincial government transfers . <p> According to DA Western Cape provincial leader Bonginkosi Madikizela , Drakenstein manages its finances so well despite not being cash flush and not being immune to challenges such as funding and revenue . An aspect the municipality is proud of is its strong record in paying creditors within thirty days of receipt of invoices . <p> " Through implementing cost containment measures , ensuring good record-keeping and with sound audit systems being in place , the municipality is able to ensure that their budgets remain credible and focussed on service delivery to the community , " said Madikizela . <p> Drakenstein is wine farming country , which , together with strong other farming activities , help ensure enviable social stability , relatively high employment figures compared to the rest of the country and ever increasing wealth levels from which municipal governance can only benefit . <p> The towns of Paarl and Wellington also boast many light industries and Paarl is a culinary centre of some repute . Tourism is an important wealth and employment creator and of late the area has experienced an influx of extremely wealthy people who have relocated to the Paarl area 's growing number of top notch luxury housing estates . <p> Paarl is the cradle of Afrikaans as a formalised language , was the birthplace of Afrikaans journalism ( the first Afrikaans newspaper ever was published in Paarl in 1876 ) and the town is home to the Afrikaans Language Monument . <p> It 's beautiful mountains , fast-flowing , clean rivers , Cape Dutch architecture and aesthetically pleasing bountiful farmland contribute to Drakenstein also being one of South Africa 's most scenic municipalities , in addition to being named financially the best managed . <p> &gt;Prepaid politics : Here 's who profited from Ramaphosa 's CR17 campaignhttps : **31;5600;TOOLONG ... African National Congress ( ANC ) has announced the names of the interim provincial leadership task team which has to attempt to rebuild the organisation in the Western Cape after its worst provincial electoral performance ever . <p> In the May 2019 election , the ANC chalked up only 27% of the vote in the only province it does not govern . Each branch must then , by order of the ANC NEC , accept an action plan to get it working again , trying to stem the tide against the ANC in a province the national leadership has tasked the group with one day winning back and governing for the green , black and gold . <p> &gt;Disconnect between business and state contributed to Marikana massacrehttps : **31;5717;TOOLONG ... Fri , 16 Aug 2019 06:25:38 +0000https : **26;5750;TOOLONG ? p=50760 ... Hamann , University of Cape Town <p> The Marikana massacre , in which 34 striking mineworkers were shot dead by police on 16 August 2012 , was a tragic and historic event in South Africa . A judicial commission of inquiry set up to investigate how it came about put much of the blame on the police . <p> It was also critical of the mining company , Lonmin . In particular , the commission highlighted the company 's failure to live up to its promise to build 5,500 houses for workers . It only built three . This created a situation , according to the commission , in which " large numbers of Lonmin workers live in squalid informal settlements ... creating an environment conducive to the creation of tension , labour unrest , disunity among its employees or other harmful conduct " . <p> Even a Lonmin executive conceded this link in one of the commission 's hearings . <p> But how was it possible for Lonmin to renege on its promise to build 5,500 houses ? After all , this was a formal commitment made in terms of the Mining Charter of 2002 and thus legally binding . <p> Based on a growing scholarly literature on corporate irresponsibility , some might see this as yet another example of " a corporate bad guy . " Others might point to the " systemic " nature of corporate irresponsibility , in which a broader array of actors are also implicated . <p> Having collected data on mining companies in this area since 2001 , I suspected that additional , processual factors were at play . So I studied how interactions between business and government created the underlying conditions that gave rise to the Marikana massacre . <h> Business -- government interactions <p> I analysed interactions between business and government in the ten-year period from the creation of the Mining Charter of 2002 to the Marikana Massacre in 2012 . The charter was meant to specify post-apartheid transformation rules for the industry . <p> I studied these interactions at the national level , especially in the debates surrounding the charter , as well as at the local level in the platinum mining area around Marikana . <p> I paid particular attention to two key aspects of governance . These were the establishment and enforcement of commonly binding rules , and the provision of public goods and services . <p> I found that interactions between business and government progressively dissipated the adopted and enacted social responsibilities of both parties . The term I used to describe the process is " dynamic de-responsibilisation . " <h> Ambiguous and flexible rules <p> The process started with the government committing to negotiate rules with companies . This was premised on assumed shared interests , as well as fears of an investment strike . But the negotiation process resulted in vague and ambiguous rules . <p> For example , the Mining Charter scorecard required that companies " cooperate in the formulation of integrated development plans and ... in the implementation of these plans " in mine-affected communities . Such a vague expectation is difficult to enforce . <p> The government and the mining companies also emphasised flexibility in the implementation of these ambiguous rules . As the government negotiator highlighted " you do n't want to straitjacket anybody , so there is flexibility in the implementation of the framework " . <p> These outcomes were at least in part due to the lobbying of the mining industry . But the lack of clear and implementable rules was not the only problem . <h> Absconding state <p> The Mining Charter , as well as a broader international debate about corporate responsibility , expected companies to contribute to public goods and services in areas near their mines . The companies accepted this . In fact , mining companies sought to outdo each other in acting , and being seen , as public development agents . <p> Local mining managers were also motivated by the need to maintain a viable operating environment in the context of a failing local state . One manager noted , " where local government is supposed to be looking after public goods and services , we are doing refuse removal , we are doing sewage plants , we are supplying quite a big portion of water " . <p> What 's more , the local state consciously absconded from areas which were expected to be supported by the mining companies . A government representative reportedly told local officials asking for bigger budgets : <p> " You , you are the children of the mine , whatever your needs are , you go and you ask the mine , do n't come here , we have lots of other communities that do n't have mines that look after them " . <p> Together , the ambiguity of the negotiated rules and the absence of the local state provided an excuse for Lonmin to renege on its housing commitment . <p> Company managers pointed out that their contributions to public goods and services around the mines were constrained by an absent local state . A senior manager in another company active in the area argued , <p> " We must not underestimate some of the serious capacity constraints within the municipalities , and the extent to which this constrains even the ability of mining companies to deliver on our own Social and Labour Plan obligations . " <p> The overarching outcome was that neither government nor Lonmin lived up to their statutory or public commitments . They failed to provide public goods to communities around the mine . This contributed to the squalor of Marikana and other settlements in the area , which in turn fed the grievances of striking mineworkers . <h> Clearly defined roles and responsibilities <p> My argument does not suggest that participation by companies in negotiated rule-making and the contribution of public goods is necessarily harmful . <p> But two things need to happen to make this work . Firstly , there must be clear and enforceable rules and companies must be monitored to ensure that they deliver on their promised contributions . <p> Secondly , government ca n't retreat . Companies should insist that their contributions to public goods and services do not displace , but are in support of , government and other state organs as the primary governance agents . <p> &gt;#WorldElephantDay horror : Video shows traumatised elephants at Joburg Zoohttps : **36;5778;TOOLONG ... Thu , 15 Aug 2019 09:44:12 +0000https : **26;5816;TOOLONG ? p=50709 ... views expressed in this article are not necessarily those of The South African . <p> Lammie 's 40th birthday celebrations and World Elephant Day at Johannesburg Zoo were marred by scenes of highly stressed elephants . <p> In a viral video shared online , Lammie is seen running out of her dark quarters , clearly confused and traumatized . The two other elephants behind bars also react in alarm , much to the misguided delight of the screaming onlookers . <p> The Zoo 's two new elephants , a 22-year-old male named Ramadiba and a 19-year-old female named Mopani are still kept separate from Lammie . Zoo spokesperson Jenny Moodley confirmed to Beeld that Lammie has been kept separate from the newcomers and only been allowed to interact with them through fences for small periods at a time . <p> On World Elephant Day , Mopani was made to do tricks to entertain the crowds of visitors bussed in for the occasion . <p> Photos show her having to lie down , then roll over in front of the media . She was also made to stand on her knees several times . The two newcomers were initially caught in the wild by EFAF as calves and trained to perform stunts for the elephant tourism industry . <p> Smaragda Louw <p> Brett Mitchell , elephant behavioural expert and chairperson of the Elephant Reintegration Trust ( ERT ) says the Zoo 's cruel experiment is typical of a facility that 's only keeping elephants for the sake of entertainment and economic gain . <p> " The elephants ' behaviour indicates they were under immense stress . " Heavy temporal streaming , running with head and tail up and foot-swinging are typical signs of distress and separation anxiety in elephants . " Lammie is seen spinning , dribbling urine and kicking the ground as soon as she is released back into the enclosure on her own . From the other two elephants , there is loud bellowing while they buckle their hind legs -- a typical sign of stress , " Mitchell says . <p> " If the Zoo had any respect for elephants , especially on World Elephant Day , they would not have created an entertainment program for people which negatively affects the very animals they claim to look after , " Mitchell says . " The Zoo once again shows their complete lack of morality and has no concern for their elephants ' welfare . Once again , this shows that entertainment trumps while the elephants ' welfare is pushed aside . <p> " The increased noise and disturbance as well as the children 's misplaced shouts of delight clearly exacerbated the situation and elevated the elephant 's stress , " Delsink says . " <p> This latest incident follows a public outcry against the keeping Lammie in the Zoo and the introduction of two new elephants . <p> The Johannesburg Zoo is suing Ban Animal Trading ( BAT ) and the National Council of SPCAs for criticizing their World Elephant Day ' celebrations ' and for sharing footage of the zoo 's traumatized elephants online . <p> BAT 's video showed the bewildered and visibly traumatised elephants in front of scores of excited visitors . It has been viewed more than 38 000 times on Facebook . <p> Photos also show one elephant performing tricks in front of the press while Lammie was kept locked away from the media for hours . <p> Joburg Zoo says the posts are defamatory . The lawyers ' letter to BAT demands that they be withdrawn and an apology be issued . The NSPCA confirms they 've received similar legal documents . <p> BAT shared the lawyers ' letter online , saying " Free the Johannesburg Zoo Elephants ! " They say it 's concerning that the zoo would use taxpayers ' money to sue Non-Profit Organisations working in the interest of animals . <p> The Zoo was previously criticized for squandering public funds when they paid almost R1 million more than the market rate for its two new elephants . <p> **26;5844;TOOLONG nuclear war : Hanekom fires straight at Zuma 's hearthttps : **34;5872;TOOLONG ... Wed , 14 Aug 2019 09:50:20 +0000https : **26;5908;TOOLONG ? p=50650 ... Tourism Minister Derek Hanekom 's decision to sue former President Jacob Zuma for R500 000 -- and his request for a High Court order requiring humiliating personal admissions and actions from Zuma -- is a full-frontal attack , strategically aimed at causing maximum damage to President Cyril Ramaphosa 's opponents in the African National Congress ( ANC ) . <p> Details about the dramatic twist in the raging war to direct ANC and government policy was first published by the newspaper Business Day . <h> Zuma and his ' spy claims ' -- Hanekom hits back <p> The court case , which is expected to start next Wednesday , sees Hanekom claiming R500 000 in defamation costs after Zuma 's recent Twitter claim that Hanekom was a spy for the apartheid regime during the liberation struggle . <p> In his legal argument , Hanekom states that he was never an agent of , or spy for , the apartheid regime , and bluntly accuses Zuma of being a liar . He demands that Zuma either proves his claims empirically or pay R500 000 for maliciously and falsely damaging Hanekom 's good name and reputation . <p> Should Zuma fail to thus prove his claim , Hanekom requests the Court to order Zuma to delete the tweet and then to " unconditionally withdraw his spy claim , acknowledge that it was entirely false " and apologise . <p> He also wants Zuma to be " interdicted from publishing any statement that says or implies " that Hanekom " is or was an enemy agent or apartheid spy " . <p> Finally , Hanekom requests the Court to order Zuma to tweet : <p> " On 25 July 2019 , I published a tweet which alleges that Mr Derek Hanekom is a known enemy agent . I unconditionally withdraw this allegation and apologise for making it as it is entirely false . I have no basis whatsoever for asserting that Mr Hanekom is a known enemy agent . " <p> Should Hanekom succeed , Zuma 's integrity and his standing in the ANC will be seriously weakened , and Ramaphosa 's opponents , including many former Ministers , Premiers and other ANC leaders will see their strongest drawcard and most powerful weapon lose much of its potency within the ANC . <h> ' A weapon of mass ( political ) destruction ' <p> Ever since the ANC 's unbanning in 1990 , Zuma has claimed that as the ANC 's intelligence chief during the armed struggle , he has irrefutable empirical proof of which ANC members -- including powerful leaders currently held in high acclaim -- were spies and agents of the apartheid regime with which the ANC was embroiled in a deadly armed struggle . <p> If Zuma has such proof and it stands up to judicial scrutiny , he controls a weapon which will destroy those thus proven to have betrayed the comrades who trusted and depended on them , thus causing the detention , torture or death of freedom fighters so betrayed . <p> And whenever accusations of callous misdeeds would surface against the former president , he would threaten to use his alleged weapon of mass destruction , thereby often deflecting attempts to hold him accountable . <p> If Hanekom 's imminent court action succeeds and Zuma proves -- by his own written admission -- to be nothing but a self-serving liar , everything the famously vicious former President claims about his opponents would be safely considered a self-serving lie by a disreputable cad unless it is upheld by a court of law . <p> Zuma would be assumed a liar unless a specific statement by him is found by a judge to -- for once -- not be a lie . <p> A situation where no decent person can believe anything its most prominent vote-winner , chief spokesperson and main figurehead says would be a huge disadvantage for Ramaphosa 's opponents , because none but the blindest , most unprincipled and most unthinking Zuma-supporting voting fodder among ANC members can be considered firm Zuma supporters . <h> Zuma caught in a lie ? Unpacking Hanekom 's history <p> If Zuma were to provide empirical truth that Hanekom was an apartheid spy or agent , the position of Ramaphosa 's opponents and of Zuma personally would be enormously strengthened . If that were to be the case , it would come as a huge shock given Hanekom 's role and ordeals at the hands of the very apartheid regime Zuma claims he served so diligently . <p> Derek Hanekom grew up in Cape Town , attending the German School and matriculating from Jan van Riebeeck High School , the Afrikaans language school serving the city 's Afrikaner establishment elite . <p> In 1978 , Hanekom and his wife , the academic Dr Trish Hanekom , moved to Magaliesburg near Johannesburg where Derek Hanekom became a vegetable , dairy and poultry farmer on a smallholding outside the town . <p> The Hanekom couple both joined the banned ANC , the mere membership of which was punishable by a jail sentence if discovered and proven by the apartheid security authorities . Despite this risk and forfeiting any normal social life in the segregated local white community , Derek and Trish Hanekom were deeply involved in ANC underground work . <p> Amongst other contributions , they provided the ANC with empirical and documentary proof of how the South African Government actively , secretly and despite its continuous public denials , stoked the deadly Mozambican civil war by arming and replenishing Renamo in its bloody battle against the Frelimo government . <p> This explosive proof that the apartheid regime was a lying warmonger causing untold suffering and casualties , proved a major victory for the ANC and a telling disaster for the regime Zuma claims Hanekom was so loyal to . <p> In 1983 , the feared security forces struck hard against Hanekom . Derek and Trish were arrested and charged with high treason , a crime punishable by death . After massive international pressure on their behalf , they were found guilty on a lesser charge . <p> Derek served three years in jail and Trish four , never having any physical contact during that period -- all of which would be well nigh unbelievably harsh treatment by people Hanekom was a cosy part of , if Zuma is to be believed . <p> Following Trish 's eventual release , she was banned to Zimbabwe where Derek joined her . The couple remained in exile in Harare until former President FW de Klerk normalised South African politics and unbanned the ANC . <p> That is the struggle history and the apparent personal suffering Zuma will have to disprove in the imminent court case , if he does not want to be proved a self-serving liar . <p> &gt;NHI South Africa : Plan to fight state take-over of doctors expected todayhttps : **34;5936;TOOLONG ... Tue , 13 Aug 2019 09:19:22 +0000https : **26;5972;TOOLONG ? p=50587 ... as the political focus is currently on the war for control of the country 's policy direction raging between President Cyril Ramaphosa , Public Enterprises Minister Pravin Gordhan and their supporters on the one hand , and Public Protector Busisiwe Mkhwebane , the EFF and their supporters on the other , we must also keep an eye on an important announcement regarding the future of National Health Insurance ( NHI ) on the other hand . <p> This afternoon , Democratic Alliance ( DA ) leader , Mmusi Maimane , will announce his party 's next steps to halt the implementation of what the DA calls " the dangerous and ill-advised NHI Bill . " <p> According to the DA , the bill in its current form threatens to not only collapse South Africa 's health sector , but places the country 's entire economy at risk . <h> Unpacking NHI in South Africa <p> The NHI is a very important and controversial government policy which aims to completely change the way healthcare is provided in South Africa . <p> In practice , it will place all medical services under state control . <p> Although it will not officially outlaw private medical practice and private medical schemes -- and while admitting that exact regulations have not been finalised -- in practice , all doctors will be paid for services rendered at a fixed price from a central medical fund , paid for by the taxpayer and administered by a civil service-type bureaucracy , which will regulate which medical services will be covered by whom at what point . <p> Supporters of the NHI claim it will bring equality in healthcare , leading to better healthcare for the vast majority of South Africans . <p> Supporters of the NHI also claim that South Africa will be joining several of the world 's leading nations , such as Britain and France , on whose public healthcare systems supporters claim the South African NHI proposal is based . <p> The policy decision to implement the NHI was taken by majority vote at the ANC 's conference at Nasrec in 2017 , together with other far-reaching decisions such as that the Reserve Bank must be nationalised and the Constitution must be changed to expedite and simplify expropriation of land without compensation . <h> Economic and political pitfalls <p> The implementation of each of all three those decisions have Ramaphosa and his supporters in a double fix , firstly economically and secondly politically . The implementation will have massive economic implications at a time when the country has basically absolutely no room for movement and no cash to pay for those policies . <p> But the Ramaphosa camp knows only too well the political risk of not implementing those ANC conference policy decisions . <p> Ramaphosa 's hold on the ANC leadership has always been precarious , at best , and his opponents within the ANC are not letting up at all . <p> In fact , they are openly organising a rebellion against him , and have given notice , through the leadership of the Durban-based eThekwini region , which has the most ANC members of any region , that a motion of no confidence will be put on Ramaphosa when the next national mass gathering of ANC branches -- the National General Council ( NGC ) -- takes place around July next year . <p> The NGC must take stock of whether the ANC national conference policy decisions are being implemented properly as instructed . The Ethekwini region 's motion of no confidence will be rooted in the claim that Ramaphosa has gone rogue -- that he is failing or even refusing to execute the policy instructions and mandate of the organisation which directs him , and therefore the ANC members who elected him have lost confidence in him . <p> The main examples of the alleged rogue behaviour which eThekwini regional ANC leaders have thus far listed are ; failure to implement the expropriation resolution , the Reserve Bank resolution and the NHI resolution . <p> The motion of no confidence , which , if carried , will be a mortal blow to Ramaphosa 's political credibility , is a clear and present danger , whatever the economic cost of implementation . <h> Strong opposition to the NHI <p> But the NHI faces very strong opposition , too . Opponents of the plan say it will destroy the only part of the health system which works , which is the private medical services . <p> It is furthermore claimed that private medical services benefit the country because although those who use it largely fund the public health system through their tax contributions , they also allow the benefit of that taxation to accrue almost entirely to the poor , because by not using the public health system , they unburden it so that it can focus entirely on the needs of the poor . <p> Opponents make two other very fundamental points . Firstly , they claim doctors will flee the country in large numbers to escape the dead hand of the state for countries where they can be free . <p> Anecdotal evidence is that the exodus has already started , which could probably leave the NHI and the country with a deadly skills deficit . <p> Secondly , opponents ask what proof there is that the state and the government has the ability to implement the NHI when it is clearly not even able to run the current public health system , which is much simpler and smaller , but which is ( even by the ANC government 's own admission through its preference for the NHI ) an unmitigated disaster . <p> It joins a list of several other government-directed disasters of which Eskom , the SABC , SAA , Denel , the land reform process and large parts of the public education system are the best-known examples . <h> Pilot projects leave much to be desired <p> On the point of the probable inability of the state to manage the NHI , the DA -- which has so far led political opposition to the NHI -- scored a major win when its energetic Shadow Minister of Health , Siviwe Gwarube , revealed the results of the NHI pilot projects the state has run thus far . <p> The government had not shared those results with the public , whose health and lives will depend upon it . The findings thus hidden were that the pilot projects were a disaster . <p> " The report details the haphazard nature in which various interventions were rolled out , lack of controls and accountability , poor planning and lack of funding in certain instances . <p> Projects were rarely implemented or completed due to lack of planning and funds not being released on time . <p> There was infrastructure deterioration because of a lack of basic maintenance , and only 350 part-time doctors were hired across all 11 projects , " the DA revealed . <p> All of which sounded very much exactly like the state 's failed management of other huge projects . In reaction , Health Minister Dr Zweli Mkhize and his department admitted that the DA information was correct , and offered no explanation why they had not informed the public of the failures . <p> They simply claimed that they had learnt from the failures , and said the phased implementation of the NHI -- of which the legal mandate is contained in the NHI bill currently being discussed by the health portfolio committee in parliament -- will go ahead . <p> This is why today 's announcement by the DA on how the bill will be opposed is so important . <p> It will help shape a fundamentally important debate on a policy proposal which may be great if implemented effectively , but which could very well create a worse national disaster than Eskom and state capture combined if implemented as poorly as the practical trial runs so evidently were . <p> The opinions expressed in this publication are those of the authors . They do not purport to reflect the opinions or views of the thesouthafrican.com . <p> &gt;Ramaphosa , Gordhan , Public Protector : Who 's winning ? https : **31;6000;TOOLONG ... Mon , 12 Aug 2019 07:26:43 +0000https : **26;6033;TOOLONG ? p=50526 ... like a ridiculously high stakes game of high-speed action chess , is n't it ? This battle between President Cyril Ramaphosa and Public Enterprises Pravin Gordhan on the one hand , and the odd bin consisting of Public Protector Busisiwe Mkhwebane , the EFF and assorted acolytes of former President Jacob Zuma on the other . <p> It would have been more enthralling had our fascination been less morbid , had the prize not seemed the future well-being of our country and had it not been so tough to say who is going to win . <p> With each side loudly proclaiming victory after every small battle , how does one know the true score ? <h> The difference between big battles and small ones <p> If you want to judge the true state of the war , it is important to distinguish between the big battles and the small ones . To do that , one needs to focus on the facts , not the personalities , one needs not to choose sides but consider each set of facts , and one needs to consider every case with its unique set of facts separately . <p> Therefore , much as it is beguiling to speculate about the true motives behind the moves of Mkhwebane ( why does she seem to fixate on this case , and not others ? ) or Gordhan ( does the media give him a free pass , and who is behind it ? ) , those are not the issues at hand . <p> Indeed , many small battles are playing themselves out currently , and they have resulted in several embarrassing judicial slap-downs for the hapless Mkhwebane , lately nicknamed " Bungling Busi " by The Sunday Times . On this issue , rule 1 is whether the Public Protector even has the right ( jurisdiction ) to investigate it , seeing as that her mandate is to investigate the use and misuse of state ( taxpayers ' ) money , whereas this concerns a campaign for a position in a political party , not government . <p> When in doubt , refer back to rule 1 . <p> Should it pass the Rule 1 test , the financial details of the campaign , but therefore maybe of every ANC leadership position campaign , seem murky at best . Something rotten in the state of Luthuli House ? <p> So , three questions to be decided on the first issue , namely can the Public Protector investigate it , what are the details and , importantly , did Ramaphosa lie to Parliament about it either in his initial answer or in the subsequent explanatory letter ? <h> Gordhan and the so-called ' SARS rogue unit ' <p> Second : Did Gordhan break any rule or regulation when an early retirement package was awarded to his long-time close comrade Ivan Pillay and said long-time close comrade was immediately rehired as a consultant at the taxpayers ' expense ? <p> Third : Did the South African Revenue Service ( SARS ) develop and use illegal spying or snooping abilities while Gordhan was in charge of it , and if so , to what degree did he act against it if and when he became aware of it ? <p> Each of these three issues is important by itself . Fri , 09 Aug 2019 21:41:38 +0000https : **26;6096;TOOLONG ? p=50462 ... phrase " men are trash " has been around for a few years now .
